# EXPANSION CW125-02 — Names Lost to the Wind

## A prose-first memorial expansion about the remembered claim, the people who inherit it, and the blanks that remain human.

### Prose Wave 125: What a Warning Cannot Finish

## Batch brief

**Content type:** original game-content proposal with full scene, attributable writing, conversation, and conditional-return drafts.
**Content bank:** 48 beat pairings; four alternative authored forms for each pairing; 192 candidates, of which a verified consumer should select only what it can support.
**Current local anchor:** memorial_radiation_storm/names_lost_to_wind — Radiation Storm Victims.
**Tone:** A collective memorial that remembers people lost in a storm through the distance between a late sheltering, unnamed dead, and a later demand to heed alarms.
**Scope:** narrative prose only; no production code, authoritative JSON edits, mechanics, route, quest, flag, simulation, or save change.

## 1. Expansion thesis

This plan explores A collective memorial that remembers people lost in a storm through the distance between a late sheltering, unnamed dead, and a later demand to heed alarms. It uses the source text “They sought shelter too late. The storm came and took them. Their names are lost to the wind, but their sacrifice reminds us: never hesitate when the alarms sound.” as a narrow canon anchor, not as an excuse to manufacture witnesses or explain every silence. Six movements and forty-eight beat pairings explore how different present readers may respond to the wording. The proposed passages remain optional editorial drafts until a current owner and route are verified.

## 2. Story question

How can a story honor the remembered loss without converting hindsight into a procedure, a hazard tutorial, or a guarantee that urgency always has one clear answer?

## 5. Human center

An alarm heard after the fact, when the memorial has hindsight but its subjects had only the moment they lived through.
 The deceased or collective subject must never become a device whose only purpose is to teach a clean lesson to the living. Keep the person or people larger than the inscription’s most repeatable line.

## 6. Voice and point of view

- **A keeper of the memorial text:** Reads the imperative as grief shaped into a sentence, not an instruction manual.
- **A listener who remembers delay:** Speaks only about a proposed present feeling, not a sourced event.
- **A questioner:** Asks what an alarm can and cannot promise to someone hearing it.

The people listed here are proposed present-day readers, not canon characters and not witnesses unless another verified source establishes that history. Keep the original memorial voice distinct from any reply written for this plan.

## 8. Player agency

The player may read, ask who authored a claim, hear a second interpretation, decline to discuss it, or leave without a verdict. These are editorial postures rather than promised controls. Silence must remain a complete response. Do not attach approval, trust, reputation, item, resource, treatment, memorial completion, or relationship state to the reader’s interpretation.

## 9. Continuity, dignity, and safety

Do not name, count, diagnose, locate, or stage the victims; the source gives no exact storm timing, exposure, dose, alarm system, shelter, or medical outcome. Do not give radiation safety advice, imply that one alarm always resolves uncertainty, or present the memorial as a real-world hazard guide. Treat “never hesitate” as retrospective memorial language, not a universal procedure.
 Keep the source statement, a proposed speaker’s interpretation, and any verified world fact visibly separate. Avoid reenacting death, assigning fault without evidence, or converting grief into a tutorial.

## 11. Narrative sequence

These six editorial movements organize the prose bank; they are not six required visits, a chronology, or a quest chain. A verified consumer may use a single beat, a short excerpt, or none of the candidates.

### Movement 1: The Imperative Comes Later

The reader encounters a command that was written after the loss it invokes. In this movement the candidate question is: How does hindsight change the sound of an instruction? The memorial’s phrase “sought shelter” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 2: Shelter as a Word

The record names shelter without identifying a place or method. In this movement the candidate question is: What can the word shelter carry without becoming a map? The memorial’s phrase “too late” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 3: Too Late

A short judgment collapses an unknown interval into two words. In this movement the candidate question is: Who can know the exact moment when late began? The memorial’s phrase “the storm came” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 4: Wind and Missing Names

The memorial admits that its roll of memory is incomplete. In this movement the candidate question is: Can a collective tribute make room for absence without filling it with invented names? The memorial’s phrase “took them” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 5: Sacrifice and Unequal Knowledge

The word gives meaning to a loss without showing what each person understood. In this movement the candidate question is: When does a public explanation exceed the evidence? The memorial’s phrase “names are lost to the wind” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

### Movement 6: The Alarm Afterward

A later reader considers the command without receiving a mechanic’s script. In this movement the candidate question is: How can urgency be remembered without promising certainty? The memorial’s phrase “sacrifice” gives the reader a concrete point of attention, while the record remains a public statement rather than a complete witness account. The scene should let people disagree about meaning without asking the player to approve one interpretation.

## 12. Beat bank and alternative passage drafts

Every movement meets each of eight source phrases. The four forms per pairing are alternatives: an observable scene, an attributable reader-authored fragment, a two-voice conversation, and a conditional return. Their headings help writers compare form; they do not promise that all versions fit one consumer. Dialogue is deliberately from proposed present readers, not from the person memorialized.

### Beat 01: The Imperative Comes Later × sought shelter

**Beat question:** When a reader meets “sought shelter” during “The Imperative Comes Later,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that the record reports an effort, without describing its timing or form?

#### Scene draft 001 — sought shelter — The Imperative Comes Later

This version starts in the small interval between recognizing the record and deciding what to repeat from it. The dramatic work of “The Imperative Comes Later” comes from placing “sought shelter” beside what the memorial does not say. No invented detail is allowed to close that gap. The proposed reader is not a witness to the life being memorialized; their presence only gives the source wording a present audience.

Let the focus settle on “sought shelter”. The phrase has force because the record reports an effort, without describing its timing or form. A second person notices that force before anyone turns it into an explanation. They may look toward the line, then toward the person who has stopped reading, giving the beat a human action rather than an invented flashback.

A reader who dislikes the confidence of hindsight begins with a modest response: “I can read sought shelter, but I cannot tell from this sentence everything that happened. I want to keep the wording because it matters to someone, and I want to leave room where the record leaves room.” The line is written as present interpretation, not a quotation falsely attributed to the person on the memorial.

A memorial keeper who will not invent the missing names replies, “Then let us ask what it asks of us now. When a reader meets “sought shelter” during “The Imperative Comes Later,” what can they honor in that wording while remembering that the record reports an effort, without describing its timing or form? I can honor the claim and still notice what it does not tell us.” The answer should be specific to the source phrase; it must not flatten the disagreement into a lesson about all grief or all survivors.

A third voice, a person who hears an alarm in the present but knows no procedure from this text, may interrupt gently. They do not correct the catalog or add evidence. They ask whether the word “sought shelter” is being repeated because it comforts the room, because it warns the room, or because no one knows what else to say. Let more than one motive remain possible.

The scene’s emotional turn around “sought shelter” in “The Imperative Comes Later” is small: one listener stops trying to finish the missing story for a moment. A chair shifts, a copied line is turned face down and then face up again, or the speaker chooses a less certain verb. The selected gesture should fit the verified presentation context; no physical memorial site is established by this proposal.

For “sought shelter” in “The Imperative Comes Later,” player-facing tone stays open. The player may read, ask for the source wording again, decline the conversation, or simply listen. These are writing possibilities, not a promised menu or branching system. No answer earns approval, trust, resources, safety, reputation, or a new relationship state.

The closing breath belongs to the living reader; do not fabricate an answer on behalf of the person commemorated. The last attention returns to “sought shelter”, now heard through the live disagreement rather than treated as a complete biography.

## 13. Player-facing tone

Keep sentences close to the source wording and the present reader’s actual knowledge. Allow one voice to value the memorial and another to object to its universal-sounding conclusion. Neither reaction should be framed as the correct morality. Use quiet material action rather than explanatory narration. Avoid turning illness, hunger, conflict, radiation, cold, machinery, duty, or trust into a puzzle tutorial or instruction to the player.

## 14. Editorial acceptance

Do not name, count, diagnose, locate, or stage the victims; the source gives no exact storm timing, exposure, dose, alarm system, shelter, or medical outcome. Do not give radiation safety advice, imply that one alarm always resolves uncertainty, or present the memorial as a real-world hazard guide. Treat “never hesitate” as retrospective memorial language, not a universal procedure.
 Re-check the exact local record and any newer narrative data before selecting text. Retain only passages that the current consumer can reach and attribute honestly. Shortening a candidate is preferable to implying unsupported canon.

## 15. Character and relationship continuity

The proposed roles are anonymous present-day readers. Do not promote them into named survivors, historical witnesses, patients, faction leaders, victims, or permanent companions without a separate source and authorized content decision. Their relationships exist only within the selected text. Gratitude, skepticism, grief, forgiveness, caution, and disagreement do not become saved relationship values.

## 16. Passage-selection map

**Use a scene** when a verified reader-facing owner can stage a present response without claiming a sourced location. **Use a record** when existing content supports authorship and provenance. **Use a conversation** when a second perspective adds a genuine question rather than repeating the thesis. **Use a return vignette** only if the existing route already supports the callback condition; otherwise use its non-conditional final line or omit it. These are writing choices, not feature requests.
